Title: Barron Optimal Transport I: Generative Modeling

URL Source: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.10875v1

Published Time: Fri, 09 Oct 2026 00:27:55 GMT

Number of Pages: 145

Markdown Content:
# Barron Optimal Transport I: Generative Modeling 

Evan Dogariu and Joan Bruna Courant Institute School of Mathematics, Computing, and Data Science New York University October 9, 2026 

Abstract 

Motivated by recent applications in generative modeling and sampling, we introduce a frame-work for optimal measure transport where cost captures the notion of neural network complexity. In transport-based generative models, samples from a reference distribution (e.g. Gaussian) are mapped to samples of a target distribution along ordinary or stochastic differential equations. These are implemented as deep residual networks when discretized in time, where each hidden layer approximates the associated instantaneous velocity. Thus, given a pair of target and ref-erence measures, a natural question is to search for the most efficient neural representation that implements this transport. Our starting point is the kinetic formulation of OT, due to Benamou and Brenier. We replace the average kinetic L2 energy by the Barron energy [7, 31], a natural norm which measures the complexity of representing a given vector field with a neural hidden layer, and which captures the adaptive properties of feature learning. This defines a metric on the space of probability measures, complementing existing Wasserstein and Stein geometries. In this work we examine the properties of this metric in the context of generative modeling. As a first application, we quantify the suboptimality of diffusion generative modeling in the Barron geometry by establishing super-polynomial score approximation lower bounds for data generated by neural network pushforwards of the Gaussian. We then investigate the benefit of adaptivity as a way to study alternative generative models. In a companion paper [28] we leverage the Barron transport geometry for sampling applications, extending the scope of Stein variational gradient methods via feature adaptation.

# 1 Introduction 

Generative modeling and sampling have become central problems in modern machine learning and scientific computing. Generative modeling – the task of using a finite dataset to learn to generate new samples from the same underlying data distribution – describes methods ranging from generative adversarial networks and diffusion/flow-matching models to autoregressive large language models (LLMs) [39, 72, 11]. Sampling – the task of using structural information about the target distribution (such as the unnormalized density or its score) to generate samples – is equally important in the computational sciences: for example, it is used to explore protein conformations and molecular free-energy landscapes [70, 62], to compute equilibrium properties of many-body systems [60], and to simulate lattice field theories [29]. Currently, the prevalent approaches in both tasks are rooted in a dynamical perspective, in which one starts with samples drawn from a simple base distribution μ (such as the standard Gaussian for continuous data or the empty string for language) and iteratively transports them during the sampling process toward a target distribution π; this is the case for flow-matching models [54, 56, 2] and diffusion models [43, 75], LLMs, and most sampling algorithms. If we restrict to continuous-time, then heuristically this dynamical transport from the base distribution to the target distribution is governed by the continuity equation 1

∂tρt + ∇ · (ρtvt) = 0 , (1) where ρ0 = μ is the base distribution, ρ1 = π is the target distribution, ( ρt)t∈(0 ,1) describe the intermediate distributions along the sampling dynamics, and vt : Rd → Rd is the instantaneous velocity field. Under appropriate regularity, these dynamics may be implemented by the differential equation ˙Xt = vt(Xt), ρt = Law( Xt), X0 ∼ μ. (2)      

> 1The dynamical transport perspective can also capture stochastic transports such as diffusion models, where (1) is replaced by a Fokker-Planck equation and (2) is replaced by a stochastic differential equation (SDE). Under enough regularity, this can also be achieved by adding ∇log ρtto vt. Our results in this paper are in the ODE setting, but the high-level ideas and many specific results transfer to the SDE setting.

2From this dynamical perspective, both generative modeling and sampling can be viewed as the task of designing an appropriate transport, parametrized by ( vt)t∈[0 ,1] , using available samples from π

in the former and structural information on its density in the latter. A key question is not merely whether one can transport μ to π dynamically, but whether one can do so using vector fields that are simple to approximate in a chosen model class. For example, one may wish to restrict vt to neural networks of bounded size or to control its norm in a fixed reproducing kernel Hilbert space (RKHS), in a smoothness/Sobolev class, or in some other structured family of functions. In order to quantify this, we may wish to penalize vt using some prescribed Banach norm ∥vt∥B. Minimizing the accumulated cost  

> ∫10

∥vt∥Bdt (3) over all transports from μ to π which satisfy (1) then measures the smallest complexity required to generate π within this model class, which informally defines an “optimal transport” relative to the complexity measure B.In modern generative modeling, it has become standard to represent the drifts vt using deep residual neural networks, where each layer can be seen as an Euler discretization of an underlying neural ODE [17, 23]. We can thus view the forward pass of each drift network vt as a sequence of updates to the sample Xt, in which case the individual layers act as drifts in their own right. Phrased differently, a discretization of the dynamics (2) where each vt is represented by a deep residual network yields nothing more than a single very deep residual network pushing μ to π.This, in turn, is nothing more than a discretization of dynamics where each drift is a single layer of a neural network. Hence, it is without loss of generality to consider dynamical transports like (1) and (2) for which each vt is a shallow neural network. Such neural transports operate on two timescales: samples evolve over longer time horizons according to the target dynamics, while microscopically they evolve according to the layers of learned drift networks. In order to study the neural network size required to transport μ to π dynamically, it is therefore natural to consider (3) with a cost B which quantifies the size of a shallow neural network representation of vt.For that purpose we instantiate B as a Barron space Bϕ [8, 7, 27], roughly defined as the convex hull of ridge functions of the form x 7 → ϕ(⟨x, θ ⟩) for an activation ϕ (see Section 3.2). This space models the feature learning of neural networks by representing functions as adaptively-chosen mixtures of these ridge functions (i.e. shallow neural networks), and the Bϕ norm of a function describes the size of its smallest shallow neural network representation. These properties make 

B = Bϕ a natural choice to study what happens when transporting measures by deep networks, or equivalently by dynamics with shallow drifts. Minimizing the accumulated cost (3) over all transports from μ to π satisfying the continuity equation (1) yields a metric DBϕ (μ, π ), which we call the Barron transport metric . In words, 

DBϕ (μ, π ) captures the size of the most efficient neural network transport from μ to π, i.e. the smallest generative model for π. The formulation of DBϕ as a dynamical optimal transport problem allows us to deploy tools from optimal transport and control theory for this analysis. This will play only a minor role in the results of this paper, but in the companion paper [28] we will study the optimal transport geometry of the metric space ( P(Rd), D Bϕ ) in detail, which will help us construct and analyze new sampling algorithms (see Section 5.3 for a preview). 

1.1 Main results 

Using the language of the Barron transport metric we can now state our main technical results, which are grouped into two categories. We first study the commonly-used denoising generative 3model, which defines a path ( ρπt )t∈[0 ,1] from γd to π as the marginal law of Xt, the time-reversal of the Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process with initial law π. Our results show that depth is needed to model the associated drifts using neural networks – in other words, when we choose the denoising path as the macroscopic behavior of the transport we require alternative dynamics on a nontrivial microscopic timescale. Afterward, we take a first step toward understanding such alternative behaviors by studying the role of feature learning in the broader class of dynamical generative models. 

Denoising transport suboptimality. Our first technical result asks whether the denoising transport from γd to a target distribution π is efficient in terms of shallow neural network represen-tation. More precisely, we ask whether the denoising 2 drifts ( vπt )t∈[0 ,1] can be efficiently represented or approximated by single-layer neural networks, and whether there exist other generative models for π whose drifts admit smaller two-layer representations. This is equivalent to asking whether 

DBϕ (γd, π ) is much smaller than ∫ 10 ∥ˆvt∥Bϕ dt , where ˆ vt is a suitable approximation of vπt . We show that the denoising transport can be very suboptimal in this regard: 

Theorem 1 (Denoising suboptimality, informal) . If π is the pushforward of γd by a ResNet of depth Θ( d) and width O(1) with bounded weights and two-layer blocks using activation ϕ, then 

DBϕ (γd, π ) ≲ d. However, the following hold: 

• (Theorem 3). There exists such a ResNet (with some ϕ) for which ∫ 10 ∥vπt ∥Bϕ dt ≳ 2d, even though we have ∥vπt ∥Bϕ < ∞ for all t.

• (Corollary 1). There exists such a ResNet (with ϕ = ReLU )3 such that for any approximate drifts ˆvt there is the implication 

∫ 10

∥ˆvt(x) − vπt (x)∥2dρ πt (x)dt ≤ ε2 =⇒

∫ 10

∥ˆvt∥Bϕ dt ≳ dΩ( ε−4/5) , (4) 

and moreover if each ˆvt is a two-layer neural network then the largest width is at least dΩ( ε−4/5)

in such cases. Here, ρπt are the marginals along the ground truth denoising path. In words, exact shallow representation of the denoising drifts can require exponential width, and shallow L2-approximation to accuracy ε can require dpoly(1 /ε ) width. 4

Despite the fact that the teacher model has width O(1), we find that achieving score-matching squared error ≤ ε2 using two-layer drift models requires width of dpoly(1 /ε ). Unlike prior works showing hardness of score-matching [73, 81], the difficulty does not come from cryptographic lower-bounds or spatial regularity issues, but instead from a direct width lower bound using the Hermite rank method of [12]. It therefore demonstrates a separation between using shallow and deep neural networks for the drifts/scores in denoising generative models, rather than general hardness. One way to interpret our result is as follows. We can view the entire generative process as a single very deep ResNet, for which the score-matching loss provides supervision at intermediate layers (see Section 5.2). From this perspective it is up to the practitioner how to allocate their computational budget, either by implementing many shallow drift models or a few deeper ones. In

Figure 1: Diagram of the trajectories ρπt and ˆ ρt connecting γd to π. The ideal reverse OU path 

ρπt is depicted in red, while the effective path obtained by regressing the target scores with deep ResNet models is depicted in purple. The paths are matched at regular intervals, while the ResNet layers are free to implement arbitrary dynamics in between. Theorem 1 suggests that this freedom is useful for parameter efficiency. the extremes these correspond to maximal supervision (a flow driven by shallow denoising drifts) and minimal supervision (a deep single-shot denoising model), respectively. Theorem 1 indicates that the former strategy can be suboptimal for the class of ResNet pushforward measures: some amount of non-denoising depth is required for efficient generative modeling of these distributions. From the two-timescale perspective, this suggests that the number of neural network layers needs to be nonnegligible with respect to the number of noise scales; see Figure 1. More generally, this construction is a toy model for a natural type of generative process in which features are revealed not by varying the noise level, but instead through nonlinear interactions of multi-index type. This motivates two directions for further work: (1) studying tradeoffs between network depth and denoising supervision and (2) designing algorithms which can learn more diverse types of generative models. We discuss future directions of research in Section 5. 

Feature learning. In light of Theorem 1’s result that neural network-based denoising transports can require non-denoising dynamics between noise scales, it is natural to ask about the properties of such alternative dynamics. To this end, our second technical result aims to understand the role of feature adaptation in generic dynamical generative models. We do so by comparing the settings where each drift is a two-layer network whose first layer is either fixed or adaptive, i.e. by comparing the kernel and feature learning regimes. For a fixed kernel k : Rd × Rd → Rd×d with RKHS Hk, we denote by DHk the Stein metric on 

P(Rd) which is obtained by the length minimization (3) with the choice B = Hk. This was studied in [30] as the Riemannian geometry on P(Rd) whose gradient flow structure generates the Stein variational gradient descent sampling algorithm (see Sections 1.2 and 2.2), but for our purposes 

DHk simply measures the cost of the most efficient transport from μ to π in terms of the RKHS norm of the drifts. The RKHS norm ∥vt∥Hk captures the hardness of representing vt using frozen features (e.g. if k is a random feature kernel) in the same way that the Barron norm ∥vt∥Bϕ captures the hardness of representing vt using two-layer neural networks with adaptive features. Hence, the comparison between DHk (μ, π ) and DBϕ (μ, π ) provides a tool to study the role of feature learning in the task of dynamical transport from μ to π.In analogy to the study of Gaussian single-index models in regression [13], we consider single-index flows with Gaussian source. We perform this comparison in both the perturbative and nonperturbative regimes: 5Theorem 2 (Feature learning comparison, informal) . The first part is essentially the vector field version of the classical feature learning separation statement that single-index functions are difficult to represent in rotationally-invariant RKHSs [7, 38], and the proof is the same (via spherical harmonics/Hermite decomposition). However, the second part implies that this separation disappears once depth is allowed. Phrased differently, the second part says that a single-index function can be efficiently represented by a deep ResNet whose blocks use the same fixed feature basis. This observation raises the question of whether depth can compensate for a lack of adaptive feature selection (particularly in dynamical measure transport), which we leave to future work. Note that these quantities DHk , D Bϕ consider an infimum over all transports from γd to π, which may be qualitatively different than the transports that are learned in practice (e.g. Wasserstein optimal transports, denoising transports, etc.). 

1.2 Related work 

Dynamical optimal transport and associated metrics. The Benamou-Brenier formulation of optimal transport [9] measures transport cost by minimizing an action subject to the continuity equation, and identifies the squared 2-Wasserstein distance between two probability measures as the minimal possible action. The textbook [5] thoroughly develops this perspective in the Wasserstein case. The survey [10] covers some more general dynamical transport metrics coming from other choices of the action, see also [33] and [32] for control-theoretic instantiations of this idea. The choice of a non-Lp dynamical transport cost has direct application to the sampling problem. In [30], a dynamical optimal transport metric is defined on P(Rd) using transport costs coming from RKHS norms; this is called the Stein geometry , since the resulting Riemannian geometry on P(Rd)is used to analyze the mean-field behavior of the Stein variational gradient descent algorithm [55] by casting it as a formal Riemannian gradient flow. Similarly, in [42] a dynamical optimal transport metric is defined using the Radon transform, and the resulting Riemannian gradient flow structure yields a sampling algorithm with better per-step computational cost.                       

> 5In other words πε= Law( Xε), where ˙Xt=v(Xt) with X0∼γdand v:Rd→Rdis a single-index vector field
> v(x) = σuϕ (⟨x, θ ⟩) for some u, θ ∈Sd−1and σ > 0.

6Dynamical optimal transport for generative modeling. Dynamical generative modeling requires choosing or learning a generative path from data. Some recent works propose to do so using Wasserstein optimal transport. [35] and [63] use optimal transport machinery to regularize neural ODEs and normalizing flow models, respectively. [79] uses optimal couplings computed over minibatches in order to learn generative models. Lastly, [56] proposes a procedure in which a generative model is iteratively “rectified” to reduce transport costs and straighten trajectories. Closer to our work, several recent papers propose alternative criteria for dynamical optimal transport based on velocity regularity with the goal of better generative modeling. The paper [80] investigates rescheduling of the Wasserstein dynamical transport (and more general straight-line interpolations), deriving a reparameterization of time which minimizes the largest Lipschitz constant of the drift fields. The papers [65] and [59] propose to optimize dynamical transports using RKHS penalties on their velocity fields: the former derives some sample and model complexity bounds in this setting, while the latter highlights the optimal control perspective and practical algorithmic considerations. While researchers are beginning to design and study non-Wasserstein dynamical optimal transports for generative modeling, it remains unclear how the geometry of a target distribution relates to the notion of drift regularity we impose. 

Approximation theory for deep ResNets and neural ODEs. There is a growing body of work which studies the approximation power of deep ResNets and their continuous-time/infinite-depth limits, neural ODEs (see [23] for a quantitative study of this convergence). One approach is based on defining function spaces with suitable norms or metrics. The paper [31] notes that classical Barron spaces characterize shallow representations and do not explicitly encode depth or compositional structure; the authors subsequently design flow-induced function spaces (via neural ODE representations with sufficient regularity across depth) and prove direct and inverse approximation theorems for residual networks along with statistical complexity bounds. Related approaches include the ResNet norm of [45] and its connection to circuit complexity as well as the study of minimum-norm representation paths in [46]. These works focus on understanding which functions can be represented and approximated using ResNets and neural ODEs, largely by emphasizing the role of depth and the ODE structure. Complementary work examines which maps neural ODE architectures can represent; see [15, Section 2] and [50]. These results explain the role of augmentation and geometric restrictions, including the nonsingular Jacobian of finite-time flows generated by smooth vector fields. Such representability results do not by themselves give bounds on the norm or network size needed to approximate a particular measure transport. Relatedly, the work [67] investigates the universal approximation property for neural ODEs through control-theoretic arguments, and [52] shows that ODEs with two-layer neural network drifts can approximate a wide class of dynamics or endpoint mappings. There is also a recent line of work which specifically investigates the use of neural ODEs in the representation and approximation of probability distributions via dynamical transport. In [57] they establish statistical convergence guarantees for likelihood-trained neural ODEs, including nearly minimax-optimal density-estimation rates under smoothness assumptions. Their companion paper [58] constructs straight-line flow representations for suitable classes of smooth transport maps and derives explicit neural ODE approximation bounds in Wasserstein distance and KL divergence, with dependence on the dimension and regularity of the source and target densities. To summarize, the aforementioned works develop approximation theories for deep ResNets and neural ODEs, both for function approximation and recently in the context of probability measure approximation. These methods can yield approximation rates in the spirit of the shallow neural 7network theory, but it is still unclear which geometric properties of a data distribution allow neural ODE transports with few parameters. 

Optimality of reverse Ornstein-Uhlenbeck transport. It is known that the reverse OU probability flow (which is one of the most common approaches to generative modeling, see Section 2.3) does not always minimize the Benamou-Brenier action associated with Wasserstein optimal transport. In [77] it is shown that the endpoint map of the related reverse heat flow transport of Kim-Milman [49] can differ from the Brenier map, see also [51] for quadratic transport cost suboptimality. However, from [61] it is also known that these reverse heat flow transports have endpoint maps with good spatial regularity. These results leave open the question of how reverse OU transport compares under costs that measure the approximation/learning complexity of its velocity field. There are some notable recent works demonstrating some hardness of learning reverse OU transports. The paper [73] leverages cryptographic hardness to show the hardness of learning L2-approximate scores; in that construction the hardness comes from the learning problem, and the author notes that the true scores are easy to approximate with neural networks. The recent work of [81] constructs distributions for which (1) there exists an L2 approximate drift which does not sample well and (2) no poly-time drift estimator with controlled Lipschitz constant can sample well. Our approximation lower bounds are more structural as they do not rely on regularity or computational hardness: the Hermite rank argument directly lower bounds network size irrespective of the estimator’s regularity or computational complexity. 

Feature learning in RKHS and Barron spaces. For a common dictionary of neurons, an RKHS fixes a feature distribution, while a Barron norm optimizes over representations and can therefore reflect features adapted to the target. In [7], this distinction is studied through variation-norm regularization in order to prove adaptation to unknown low-dimensional structure, i.e. feature learning . The separation between kernel methods and neural networks is further investigated in [38] and [82] using tools from spherical harmonic analysis. These works analyze feature learning separations in the shallow regression setting. Deep and compositional kernels have also been studied [69, 53], with particular focus on the effect of depth on the spectrum and complexity of their associated RKHSs. These works demon-strate improved modeling properties arising from the compositional structure, but it is still unclear to what extent depth can compensate for the lack of adaptive feature selection in a fixed-feature RKHS. This question remains poorly understood not only in the setting of regression using deep models composed of RKHS layers, but also in the context of dynamical measure transport with RKHS drifts. 

1.3 Statement of AI Usage 

Several mathematical ideas and proof arguments in this paper were suggested by ChatGPT 5.6 and Astra and subsequently developed and refined by the authors. These include the sharp scaling of RKHS tangent norms in Lemma 10 and the tanh deconvolution argument in Lemma 17. For the tail control of the averaging operator in Lemma 23, the initial ideas originated with the authors, and subsequent exchanges with Astra helped develop and refine the argument. The authors take full responsibility for all results and arguments in this paper. 82 Preliminaries

F(·) := KL( ·∥ π) for a target π ∈ P 2(Rd), in which case the resulting curve ( ρt)t≥0 describes the evolution of marginal distributions along the Langevin dynamics. See Chapter 11 of [5] for full details. • ( Geodesics ). For two measures μ, ν ∈ P 2(Rd), one can describe the optimal transport (vt, ρ t)t∈[0 ,1] which minimizes (12) via the geodesic PDE 

vt = ∇φt, ∂tφt + 12 |∇ φt|2 = 0 a.e. , (15) where φt : Rd → R are the generating potentials (see e.g. [5, Theorem 6.2.10]). An important property of the Wasserstein geometry is its locality: the velocity of a gradient flow (resp. geodesic) at a point x ∈ Rd depends only on the Euclidean gradient of the generating potential ∇δF(ρt) (resp. φt) at x. This lack of cooperation between particles is part of the reason why the W2 gradient flow of KL( ·∥ π) can be simulated by the Langevin SDE, which is implemented with noninteracting particles. We emphasize that the L2(ρt; Rd) norm does not measure how complicated the function x 7 →

vt(x) is in terms of smoothness, approximation difficulty, etc. A transport ( vt, ρ t)t∈[0 ,1] which is optimal w.r.t. W2 connects two measures in a way which moves particles minimally, but by doing so it can sacrifice the spatial regularity of the induced drift fields vt.

2.2 Stein metric 

Let k : Rd × Rd → Rd×d be a symmetric positive-semidefinite matrix-valued kernel, and let Hk be its vector-valued reproducing kernel Hilbert space (RKHS). In particular, for every x, a ∈ Rd we have k(·, x )a ∈ H k and 

⟨v, k (·, x )a⟩Hk = ⟨v(x), a ⟩ (∀v ∈ H k). (16) 

Assumption k. Throughout the rest of this paper, we assume the kernel k : Rd × Rd → Rd×d is jointly continuous (w.r.t. the operator norm). 

If ( vt, ρ t)t∈[0 ,1] is a path with vt ∈ H k we may consider its RKHS energy ∫ 10 ∥vt∥2 

> Hk

dt in the spirit of (12), in which case the corresponding transport distance is 

DHk (μ, ν )2 := inf  

> μ→ν
> ∫10

∥vt∥2 

> Hk

dt, (17) 10 with the infimum over solutions of the continuity equation (7) with vt ∈ H k for almost every t.Equivalently, by constant-speed reparameterization, 

DHk (μ, ν ) = inf  

> μ→ν
> ∫10

∥vt∥Hk dt. (18) Note that if ∥ · ∥ L2(ρ;Rd) ≲ ∥ · ∥ Hk (which holds for e.g. bounded kernels) then W2(μ, ν ) ≲ DHk (μ, ν ), see [30, Lemma 17]. Under suitable regularity assumptions, this construction gives an extended metric (i.e. a metric which may take the value + ∞) which is referred to as the Stein metric in the literature; see [30, Definition 15, Lemma 17] for one precise formulation. Importantly, the space ( P(Rd), D Hk ) can be equipped with a formal Riemannian geometry (since the tangent spaces Hk are Hilbert) to enable analytical methods in analogy with the Wasserstein setting. This geometry has been investigated recently in [30] as the metric on P(Rd) whose gradient flow structure induces the Stein variational gradient descent (SVGD) algorithm [55]. In particular, [30] developed the Riemannian perspective and showed the following: • ( Gradient flows ). In contrast with the Wasserstein case (14), the DHk gradient flow of F

is implemented by ( vt, ρ t)t≥0 satisfying the continuity equation (7) with 

vt(x) = −(Tk,ρ t ∇δF(ρt))( x), (19) where Tk,ρ t : L2(ρt; Rd) → L2(ρt; Rd) is the integral operator with kernel k and measure ρt.6

An important special case is with F(·) := KL( ·∥ π) for a target π ∈ P (Rd), in which case the resulting curve ( ρt)t≥0 describes the evolution of marginal distributions along the mean-field limit of continuous-time SVGD. See Lemma 11 and Corollary 13 of [30] for these statements. • ( Geodesics ). For two measures μ, ν ∈ P (Rd), one can describe a transport ( vt, ρ t)t∈[0 ,1] 

which is stationary for the energy (17) by the PDE 

vt = Tk,ρ t ∇φt, ∂tφt + ⟨∇ φt, Tk,ρ t ∇φt⟩ = 0 a.e. , (20) where φt : Rd → R are the generating potentials, see [30, Proposition 20]. This can be contrasted with the Wasserstein case (15). The difference between the Wasserstein and Stein geometries is easily understood through the tangent norms: 

Wasserstein : ∥vt∥L2(ρt;Rd), Stein : ∥vt∥Hk . (21) The first measures how far/fast particles move, while the second measures how large the velocity field is in a prescribed RKHS. Crucially, the introduction of a kernel k forces particles in different locations to interact in order to decrease the RKHS norm; this can be also seen through the gradient flow and geodesic identities (19) and (20), in which the kernel integral operator Tk,ρ couples the velocities experienced by different particles. This interaction (and its implementable form using Stein integration by parts) yields the advantages of the SVGD sampling algorithm. Any choice of kernel k : Rd × Rd → Rd×d induces its own Stein geometry through the metric 

DHk , which can be understood in two equivalent ways. One perspective is that the kernel defines a notion of vector field complexity via its RKHS Hk, which yields a Benamou-Brenier-type energy minimization via (17). The other perspective is that the kernel couples spatial positions and   

> 6In other words, ( Tk,ρ v)( x) = ∫
> Rdk(x, y )v(y)dρ (y).

11 the velocities experienced by particles at those positions via the operator Tk,ρ . Of course, this interaction and the resulting quantities/algorithms depend heavily on the choice of kernel. Some common choices are bilinear kernels k(x, y ) = (1 + ⟨x, y ⟩)1d, Gaussian/RBF kernels 

k(x, y ) = e−∥ x−y∥2

1d, and the like [55]; these introduce isotropic interactions between particles. As is the case in kernel-based learning methods, much can be achieved if one were able to select the “perfect kernel” for the given data. However, in practice one must choose a kernel in advance, in which case the kernel captures a fixed set of features and ought not to prefer any particular direction. We will focus on the following class: 

Definition 1 (Rotation-equivariant kernel) . A matrix-valued kernel k is rotation-equivariant if 

k(Qx, Qy ) = Qk (x, y )Q⊤ (∀x, y ∈ Rd) (22) 

for every orthogonal matrix Q ∈ O(d). We denote the class of such kernels by Krot .

We emphasize that in practice (sampling with SVGD, learning dynamical transports via kernel methods or neural networks in the kernel regime, etc.) the kernels involved tend to be rotation-equivariant [55]. Informally, Krot can be viewed as the class of kernels which are “data-agnostic” since they represent a fixed basis-independent feature set and cannot know any features of the data. One of the main developments of this work will be the construction of a geometry on P(Rd) using Barron norms instead of RKHS norms, in which case the induced geometry can be understood as “adaptively choosing features based on the data”. This perspective will yield dynamical transports and algorithms which leverage feature learning, and one contribution of this work will be the comparison between fixed features in the rotation-equivariant Stein case and learned features in the Barron case. We defer this construction until Section 3. 

2.3 Generative models as dynamical transports 

So far, we have discussed the geometry induced by minimization over dynamical transports from 

μ to ν for an arbitrary pair μ, ν ∈ P (Rd). While these optimal transport constructions are math-ematically meaningful on their own, we are interested in these geometries as analytical tools to study the task of generative modeling. 7 In this subsection we frame generative modeling as the learning of a dynamical transport ( vt, ρ t)t∈[0 ,1] in order to draw practical conclusions from these geometric quantities. Let μ be a base distribution which is easy to sample from (such as the standard Gaussian γd), 8

and let π be the target data distribution. For our purposes, a (continuous-time) generative model consists of a velocity field ( vt)t∈[0 ,1] such that the ODE ˙Xt = vt(Xt), X0 ∼ μ, (23) satisfies Law( X1) = π. This is the standard setup of a flow model [17, 41], and the continuity equation induced by (23) can also describe the marginals of a stochastic interpolant or SDE-based generative model via its probability flow ODE. Thus, the goal of the task of generative modeling is to learn a suitable ( vt)t∈[0 ,1] using samples from the target π.The endpoint measures do not determine the path: different generative models may connect the same μ and π using very different intermediate distributions and velocity fields. For example, Wasserstein optimal transport chooses a path minimizing (13), while flow-matching and diffusion   

> 7In the companion paper [28] we will also leverage the same constructions to study the related task of sampling.
> 8Generally μwill be rotationally-invariant, since it must also be data-agnostic and ought not to depend on the choice of basis.

12 methods usually prescribe a convenient interpolation and learn the velocity field associated with (the reversal of) that interpolation. Hence, the learning problem is not well-specified unless one chooses a particular transport between μ and π to attempt to learn. We summarize the main choices below: 

Reverse Ornstein-Uhlenbeck transport. The main prescribed path studied in this paper is the denoising path to a target π ∈ P (Rd) used by modern diffusion models. The Ornstein-Uhlenbeck (OU) semigroup ( Pτ )τ ≥0 acts on functions according to

These marginals ( ρt)t∈[0 ,1] are the same as those of the Anderson reverse SDE of a time-change of the OU process, see Section 5.1 of [1]. Given target samples X1 ∼ π, we can sample the interpolation (25) directly. Moreover, if

(32) which can be learned using the finite dataset of target samples. Equivalently one may regress the Gaussian noise or score. This gives the usual score-matching objective used in (denoising) diffusion models, see [72, 43, 75, 1]. 13 Importantly, the usual score, noise-prediction, denoising, reverse-SDE, and probability flow pa-rameterizations of this Gaussian denoising path are equivalent up to explicit affine transformations and time-dependent scalar factors. For concreteness, we work throughout with the probability flow velocity in (27), and we refer to the induced transport ( vt, ρ t)t∈[0 ,1] with ρ0 = γd and ρ1 = π as the 

reverse OU transport to π. It is known that the reverse OU transport is not Wasserstein-optimal [77, 51], and in this work we will ask whether this prescribed transport is efficient in a neural network function space. 

Flow-matching transport. More generally, one may prescribe an interpolation between the base and target distributions for which the samplewise velocity is known (or estimated via finite-differences). If Xt denotes the interpolation and ˙Xt its samplewise velocity, then the marginal velocity field 

vt(x) = E[ ˙Xt | Xt = x] (33) satisfies the continuity equation for ρt = Law( Xt). The field vt can again be learned by a square-loss regression using samples of ( Xt, ˙Xt). This is the basic flow-matching construction [54, 3, 1]. 

Wasserstein optimal transport. One can instead consider the transport ( vt, ρ t)t∈[0 ,1] from μ to 

π given by the induced W2 optimal transport, i.e. the minimizer of (12). While this transport has some useful properties (particles move in straight lines, which can be helpful for discretization of the generative process), it can be hard to directly learn from target samples since the exact Wasserstein coupling is not directly available. Practical flow-matching methods therefore approximate it (for example using minibatch optimal transport [79]), after which coupled points may be connected with linear interpolations and the drifts may be learned via flow matching. A common alternate dynamical approach, dubbed rectified flow [56], is to first learn a generative model from samples using something like the score-matching objective (as described above). One then applies a rectification procedure which produces increasingly straight trajectories without increasing the Wasserstein transport cost. In practice, this can provide useful approximations to 

W2 optimal transports. 

# 3 Barron Transport Metric 

In the previous section, we framed the task of generative modeling as learning a collection of drifts (vt)t∈[0 ,1] which transport a known base measure μ (often the standard Gaussian γd) to the target data distribution π via the continuity equation (7). We surveyed two different notions of dynamical optimal transport (Wasserstein and Stein) which describe the “least complex” transport from μ to 

π, where complexity of vt is measured in L2(ρt) in the Wasserstein case and in Hk in the Stein case. The Wasserstein construction is natural when the goal of an efficient transport is to minimize the movement/speed of particles, and the Stein construction is natural to describe coherent transports which are efficient with respect to a fixed set of features encoded in the kernel. However, since we will in practice be representing and approximating the drifts vt via neural networks, it makes sense to measure a transport’s complexity using a function-space norm adapted to neural-network approximation. This will be the subject of this section: in short, we define a metric on P(Rd) which asks “what is the minimal neural network size required to transport one measure to another”? To do so, we will leverage the notion of Barron spaces [31]. 14 3.1 Abstract definition 

Before doing so, we first show that the dynamical length minimizations of (13) and (18) can be generalized using any Banach norm (satisfying a mild regularity condition, see Assumption 1) to yield an extended metric on P(Rd): 

Definition 2. Let B be a Banach space of functions from Rd → Rd which satisfies Assumption 1. For every μ, ν ∈ P (Rd), define the quantity 

DB(μ, ν ) := inf  

> μ→ν
> ∫10

∥vt∥Bdt, (34) 

where the infimum is over (vt, ρ t)t∈[0 ,1] transporting μ to ν via the continuity equation (7). 

In this work we will only prove the simplest properties of these metrics: 

Proposition 1. (P(Rd), D B) is an extended metric space (i.e. the metric may take the value +∞), and convergence in DB implies narrow convergence of measures. Proof. See Section A.1. In the companion paper [28] we study a more general setting where the tangent norm B is allowed to depend on ρt, in which case we give a thorough treatment of absolutely continuous curves, geodesics, and metric gradient flows using the methods of Ambrosio-Gigli-Savar´ e [5].

16 The Barron norm is an atomic variation norm whose atoms are normalized individual neurons; finite two-layer networks correspond to finite atomic decompositions, while the Barron space permits arbitrary mixtures. Unlike an RKHS with a fixed feature distribution, the Barron norm allows the relevant features to be chosen adaptively. As we will see, this DBϕ metric can (1) help us understand the dynamical transport of measures using two-layer mean-field neural network drifts and (2) produce interesting adaptive sampling algorithms. The first point is the main subject of this paper, while the second point will be the subject of the companion paper [28]. In general Bϕ is a Banach rather than Hilbert space, so unlike the Stein geometry there is no canonical Riemannian structure which we use here. Despite this, the spaces ( P(Rd), D Bϕ ) enjoy many dynamical properties akin to those of W2 and DHk ; the companion paper develops the metric geometry of these spaces in greater detail. 

3.3 Interpretation as a ResNet norm 

The Barron transport metric has a direct interpretation in terms of deep residual networks (ResNets). Recall that a ResNet with L residual blocks can be written in the form 

hℓ+1 (x) = hℓ(x) + 1

L vℓ(hℓ(x)) , (ℓ ∈ { 0, . . . , L − 1}), (43) where each vℓ : Rd → Rd is represented by a neural network. As L → ∞ , under some regularity this yields the Euler discretization of the neural ODE 

˙Xt = vt(Xt), (t ∈ [0 , 1]) (44) under the relabeling t ↔ ℓ/L . This continuous-depth interpretation of ResNets as neural ODEs is standard, see e.g. [31] and the quantitative L → ∞ convergence theory of Chizat [23]. Suppose that the residual blocks vℓ are two-layer networks with activation ϕ. A natural intrinsic complexity measure for the ResNet is 1

L

> L−1∑
> ℓ=0

∥vℓ∥Bϕ . (45) In the continuous-depth limit this becomes  

> ∫10

∥vt∥Bϕ dt. (46) Thus, the quantity appearing in the definition of DBϕ is exactly the continuous-depth Barron ResNet norm of the corresponding neural ODE. This also has a direct interpretation in terms of network parameters. For example, in the case of a bounded activation, if 

vℓ(x) = 

> mℓ∑
> j=1

∥vℓ∥Bϕ ≤

DBϕ (μ, ν ) = inf  

> μ→ν
> ∫10

∥vt∥Bϕ dt (49) 17 can be interpreted as the minimum continuous-depth ResNet norm required to transport μ to ν

using a neural ODE with two-layer blocks. This is the main reason we use the Barron transport geometry to study generative modeling: 

DBϕ (μ, ν ) measures the complexity of the simplest neural dynamics connecting the two distribu-tions, rather than the amount by which particles move or the complexity of the dynamics in a fixed feature space. In particular, the resulting DBϕ balls around μ consist of those measures which admit a neural network generative model of bounded size in the following sense: • Lower bounds on DBϕ (μ, ν ) correspond to lower bounds on the average Barron norm of each layer of any deep ResNet/neural ODE which transports μ to ν.• Conversely, upper bounds on DBϕ (μ, ν ) prove the existence of a deep ResNet/neural ODE transporting μ to ν whose layer-averaged Barron norm is upper bounded. Thus, bounds on DBϕ (μ, ν ) provide a quantitative understanding of the approximation complexity for dynamical transport from μ to ν using neural ODEs. 

Question 1. One could also define a finite-depth distance via D(L) 

> Bϕ

(μ, ν ) := inf 1

> L

∑Lℓ=1 ∥vℓ∥Bϕ ,where the infimum is over blocks (vℓ)Lℓ=1 s.t. F#μ = ν with F := ( 1 + 1 

> L

vL) ◦ . . . ◦ (1 + 1 

> L

v1).Quantifying the convergence D(L)

→ DBϕ as L → ∞ is useful to transfer our DBϕ results to real-world ResNets, which we leave for future work. 

Our perspective of a continuous-depth ResNet norm via a geometry on P(Rd) with tangent norm ∥ · ∥ Bϕ is complementary to the approaches of [31, 45]. These works study the approximation properties of deep ResNets by carefully designing function spaces that see the whole ResNet as a single function, which requires some way to capture the sensitivity of function outputs w.r.t. changes at intermediate layers. In [31] this is needed to control the L2 approximation error of finite-width ResNets, since error accumulates across depth in a way that leads to a path norm/multiplicative norm. In [45] this is used to describe circuit complexity of a function in terms of its ResNet representations. By contrast, the DBϕ metric leverages the energy-minimization perspective of dynamical optimal transport with fixed endpoints to encode depth, even though the cost itself is local and additive across depth. 

Remark 3. The relaxation to measures may also be useful beyond generative modeling in the study of ResNet approximation theory. For example, any infinitely-deep and infinitely-wide ResNet (i.e. a neural ODE) with bounded activations which interpolates a dataset whose inputs have marginal distribution μ and whose outputs have marginal distribution ν must have norm at least DBϕ (μ, ν )

(which is ≳ W2(μ, ν )). By using the results of [23], one should be able to get an analogous statement for ResNets of finite depth and width which have been trained for finite time. Alternatively, by using the results of [45] one can hope to understand the minimal circuit complexity needed for such a task. This offers an approach to ResNet approximation lower bounds in terms of the geometry of the data distributions themselves, which we leave for future work. 10 

> 10 Note that such an approach discards information about any coupling between inputs and outputs. Lower bounds would therefore be weaker, as they would capture the difficulty of transforming one distribution into another (as opposed to transforming each input into its corresponding output). The power of this perspective is that by relaxing to measures, we can naturally encode model depth through continuity equation dynamics while still using simple Barron technology.

18 3.4 Comparisons with other notions of distance 

As we increase the regularity of ϕ we also increase the regularity of functions in Bϕ, which can cause the distance DBϕ to increase. A useful first question to ask is how strong the DBϕ distance is under regularity assumptions on ϕ, since this can help contextualize any quantitative results we obtain regarding DBϕ . We note here a particularly clean lower bound on the Barron transport metric DBϕ

in terms of either Wasserstein distance or KL divergence: the latter is interesting since it forgets all geometric information about a target distribution. 

Proposition 2 (Wasserstein and KL lower bounds) . Let ϕ : R → R satisfy Assumption ϕ and suppose that ∥ϕ∥∞ ≤ M < ∞. Then, there is the Wasserstein lower bound 

DBϕ (μ, ν ) ≥ W∞(μ, ν )

M (∀μ, ν ∈ P (Rd)) . (50) 

If we assume further that ϕ is M -Lipschitz then there is the KL lower bound 

DBϕ (γd, π ) ≥ 14M · min  

> {

KL( π∥γd)

√d ,

> √

KL( π∥γd)

> }

(∀π ∈ P (Rd)) . (51) 

Proof. See Section A.2. Similar lower bounds hold for any DB metric with B ↪→ L∞ and B ↪→ W 1,∞,respectively, as well as with γd replaced by any log-smooth measure with sufficient tail control, see Lemma 3. This result helps us understand the foliation structure of ( P(Rd), D Bϕ ) in the case of bounded ϕ,since any measures μ, ν which have infinite W∞ distance (or KL under Lipschitzness and sufficient regularity of either μ or ν) also have infinite DBϕ distance. For example, DBϕ (μ, ν ) = + ∞ if μ has compact support but ν does not, if μ and ν are mutually singular, if μ and ν have different tail scales at infinity, etc. 

3.5 Optimal transport distances between Gaussians 

In some of our main results, we will be comparing the Stein DHk and Barron DBϕ metrics to gain some intuition about the complexity of generative modeling. In order to perform an initial comparison between these distances, we will inspect the case where both endpoints are Gaussians. If we restrict to bilinear kernels k and linear activations ϕ, the spaces Hk and Bϕ consist of affine drifts and so a Gaussian initial measure remains Gaussian along every finite-length admissible flow. This reduces the infinite-dimensional minimization in (36) to a tractable finite-dimensional matrix control problem, for which we can get matching upper and lower bounds. For further simplicity we restrict one of the endpoints to be the standard Gaussian γd, leading to: 

Proposition 3. Let π := N (m, Σ) with Σ ≻ 0. Then, the following hold up to universal constants: 

• (Wasserstein). W2(γd, π ) = 

> √

∥m∥2 + ∥Σ1/2 − 1d∥2 

> F

.

• (Stein). DHk (γd, π ) ≍ log(1 + ∥m∥) + ∥ log Σ ∥F for k(x, y ) = (1 + ⟨x, y ⟩)1d.

• (Barron). DBϕ (γd, π ) ≍ log(1 + ∥m∥) + ∥ log Σ ∥∗ for ϕ(z) = z.Here, ∥ · ∥ F is the Frobenius norm and ∥ · ∥ ∗ is the nuclear norm. 

19 Proof. This follows from Lemmas 4, 5, and 6. Interestingly, the transports realizing the upper bounds for linear Stein and linear Barron are almost the same. They move the mean and covari-ance in separate motions: the mean movement uses a spatial preconditioning by mm ⊤ to get the logarithmic dependence, while the log-covariance linearly interpolates from 0 to log Σ. See Remark 12 for more details. Compared to the Wasserstein distance, the linear Stein and Barron distances have improved dependence on m (and Σ if Σ ⪰ 1d). On the other hand, if there is a covariance eigenvalue σ2 ≪ 1then linear Stein and Barron blow up like log 1 

> σ2

while Wasserstein stays bounded. Furthermore, we have DHk ≲ DBϕ with qualitatively similar behaviors. 

Remark 4. Note that by Proposition 2, if ∥ϕ∥∞ < ∞ then DBϕ (γd, π ) = + ∞ whenever Σ̸ = 1d,and when the covariances are equal we have DBϕ (γd, π ) ≳ ∥m∥. By contrast, Proposition 3 shows that an activation with linear growth allows a logarithmic cost of translation and can connect two Gaussians of different covariance. Hence, using an activation with linear growth instead of a bounded one makes an affine shift exponentially easier to implement via a neural network flow (at least in the Gaussian case). 

# 4 Approximation Theory for Generative Paths 

Having introduced the Barron transport metric DBϕ , we now use it to study two approximation questions for generative modeling. In practice, we choose μ to be easy to sample from (such as the standard Gaussian γd) and ν a potentially complicated data distribution (which we will denote π). First, we compare the reverse OU transport to the Barron optimal transport in order to study the role of depth in denoising models. In particular, in Section 4.1 we ask when the reverse OU transport is optimal (up to constants) w.r.t. the Barron energy. We answer in the affirmative for centered Gaussians and single-index tilts, but in the negative for noncentered Gaussians and single-index flows (Proposition 4 and Example 1). To clarify the situation, we construct a measure 

π (a pushforward of γd by a deep narrow ResNet) for which the denoising path has exponential Bϕ

path norm, but which a simple time-varying single-index flow can easily generate and so DBϕ (γd, π )is small (Theorem 3). This suggests that the reverse OU transport is suboptimal for modeling neural network pushforwards of the Gaussian, but the obstruction we prove is in terms of exact representation and only for small OU times. To finish, we strengthen this result by constructing another neural network pushforward π for which any shallow neural network that ε-approximates the reverse OU drifts in L2 must have width dpoly(1 /ε ) (Theorem 4). Hence, any shallow score networks which achieve small score-matching loss on this data must have large width, irrespective of how they are learned. This implies that deeper drift networks are needed to enable efficient generative modeling for these data distributions. In Section 4.2 we instead aim to quantify the benefit of feature learning by comparing the Barron DBϕ distance to the Stein DHk distance, where Hk is the RKHS of a rotation-equivariant kernel k. We already saw that for Gaussian-to-Gaussian transports with linear ϕ and bilinear k they behave similarly (Proposition 3). However, there are also the classical feature learning separation results of e.g. [7, 38] which exhibit functions that are easy to represent in Barron spaces (i.e. using shallow neural networks) but are difficult to represent in rotation-invariant RKHS (i.e. using random feature models). In the generative modeling setting, we show that perturbatively-small single-index pushforwards/flows can yield measures in high dimension for which the ratio DHk /D Bϕ

becomes very large (Examples 2 and 3), but that this separation breaks down in the nonperturbative regime since deep RKHS transports can model single-index flows (Example 4). In other words, 20 the classical shallow feature learning separation holds locally but fails once depth is introduced. These preliminary results suggest that RKHS-based generative models can be fairly expressive, and that a separation with neural network generative models (if it exists) might instead come from the learning procedure rather than approximation capabilities. For now the comparison between RKHS-based and neural network-based generative modeling remains poorly understood, and we consider it important to compare these paradigms from both approximation and computational perspectives. To summarize, our results point toward two themes in the approximation theory of generative modeling/dynamical measure transport: • The reverse OU/denoising transport (which is common in practice) cannot always be effi-ciently modeled using shallow neural networks, even when there exist other transports which can be. In such settings, non-denoising depth is required. • The Barron optimal transport is an efficient alternative when data has preferred direc-tions/low intrinsic dimension, and an RKHS optimal transport which leverages depth may enjoy similar benefit. Taken together, these results demonstrate that the Barron/RKHS dynamical optimal transports have desirable properties for generative modeling that the current denoising approach can fail to capture. Our study of the approximation question therefore motivates a generative modeling approach based on algorithms which (approximately) solve the DBϕ or DHk optimal transport problems; in Section 5.1 we give some heuristic algorithmic ideas, but the computational question is wide open and left for future work. 

4.1 Reverse OU (sub)optimality 

As mentioned in Section 2.3, the reverse OU transport from γd to π is a common design choice for generative models. A natural question to ask is whether this transport is efficient to implement with shallow neural networks, or whether there can exist more economical alternative transports using smaller shallow networks. Another way to phrase this question is to ask whether the reverse OU transport is optimal in terms of the DBϕ energy (36), perhaps up to multiplicative constants. To help us approach this question, we define the following quantity: 

Lemma 1 (Condition for reverse OU (sub)optimality) . Let π ∈ P (Rd) and B a Banach space of functions from Rd → Rd, and define

Hence, we can use this quantity EB(π) to understand the (sub)optimality of the reverse OU generative model for various choices of target measure π ∈ P (Rd). As before, we will consider different target measures π of increasing complexity. We start with the linear Gaussian-to-Gaussian case, followed by simple nonlinear single-index perturbations of the Gaussian, after which we look at nonperturbative pushforwards of the Gaussian by deep narrow ResNets. In each setting, we will focus on the Barron case B = Bϕ in order to draw conclusions about the implementation of score-matching diffusion/denoising models using shallow neural network drifts. 12 

We will see that for very simple data (pushforwards of the Gaussian by a small teacher neural network) the reverse OU transport can fail to see the correct relationship between coordinates, as the relevant structure is not visible by varying the noise level. In such cases, it instead uses brute force and requires very wide networks to represent or approximate the OU scores, even though there exists a small alternative generative model (namely, the teacher network). These constructions provide a good toy model of a type of obstruction which denoising models with shallow neural network drifts can encounter on simple data. Our hope is that they can (1) help us understand the structures in real-world data which influence the role of depth in denoising models and (2) guide the design of other alternative generative modeling paradigms; we leave these investigations to future work.

4.1.3 Intuition: triangular shear measures 

In the following subsections, we aim to clarify the situation with nonperturbative constructions of target distributions π ∈ P (Rd) for which the reverse OU drifts (i.e. the time-dependent scores along the OU process, see Section 2.3) are difficult to model with shallow neural networks despite the fact that DBϕ (γd, π ) is small (i.e. there are alternative generative models for π with simple shallow drifts). The high-level intuition is that there can exist compositional/hierarchical structure in the data which is not visible by varying the noise level, in which case the reverse OU transport fails to leverage it. We will show that this arises naturally for data which is drawn from a pushforward of the Gaussian by a deep neural network. A good toy example to understand this mechanism is a simple construction in which π ∈ P (Rd)is a pushforward of the Gaussian γd by a triangular shear map T : Rd → Rd of the form 

T (z, u ) := ( z, u + Ψ( z)) , (z ∈ Rd−1, u ∈ R) (59) 23 for some function Ψ : Rd−1 → R. Note that πΨ := T#γd ∈ P (Rd) has Gaussian density 

πΨ

γd

(z, u ) = exp( uΨ( z) − Ψ( z)2/2) , (60) and so the last coordinate of the Gaussian score ∇ log πΨ 

> γd

is equal to Ψ. Hence, the reverse OU drifts must model Ψ (or an OU-smoothed version of it) and so they inherit Ψ’s complexity. Heuristically, the benefit of dynamical measure transport is that it allows us to avoid learning an entire endpoint map by instead learning drifts and using composition to increase the modeling capacity; this is analogous to how deep neural networks often have improved efficiency over shallow ones [34, 78]. The beauty of the reverse OU/denoising generative model is that it reduces the task from learning the entire transport to regression of the drifts [18], which in our analogy may be viewed as a form of layerwise supervision of a deep model. However, the observation of the previous paragraph implies that for pushforwards by triangular shears like (59), the scores are as complex as the endpoint map itself. In our analogy, this means that the reverse OU supervision destroys the natural modeling benefit of depth which dynamical transport usually provides. Of course, this intuition we are describing need not hold at positive OU times τ > 0, since the actual drifts involve the scores of the OU-smoothed target P ∗ 

> τ

πΨ and may be simpler. Thus, the technical part of our task is to show that the complexity of Ψ persists for a non-negligible amount of OU time. In Section A.7 we identify two different mechanisms for this: • (Smoothness). By some elementary OU stability estimates, we show in Lemma 18 that large derivatives of Ψ yield large derivatives of the drifts for positive OU times. Although Gaussian smoothing removes oscillations quickly, we find that the size of the derivative outweighs the rate of smoothing for derivatives of order at least 3. This third derivative obstruction becomes a Barron norm obstruction for activations with bounded third derivative, in which case EBϕ (πΨ) can become large (see Corollary 2). We emphasize that this lower bound is only encountered when exactly representing the full OU path and need not show up in practice. For example, if Ψ has oscillations of frequency exp( d) then the third derivative of the score is of size exp(3 d) for OU times τ ≲ exp( −2d); with any early-stopping which is ≳ exp( −2d) this obstruction is avoided. Moreover, by Girsanov one need only learn L2-approximate drifts for accurate sampling [18], which means that an approximate generative model does not need to match the third derivatives of the score. Hence, while this mechanism suggests reverse OU suboptimality for some triangular shear measures, it leaves a lot to be desired. • (Hermite rank). In [12, Theorem 4.18], it was proved that L2 approximation of a function using two-layer neural networks encounters a quantitative width lower bound in terms of the spectral tails of the function’s Hermite coefficient tensors; this is because shallow networks of small width have low-rank Hermite coefficients. Importantly, since the Wiener chaos decom-position diagonalizes the OU operator it is easy to track the evolution along the OU flow. Thus, for Ψ which have heavy Hermite spectral tails across Hermite degrees, we can show that a low score matching loss with shallow network drifts implies large width (see Proposition 9). Unlike the smoothness obstruction, this lower bound has more practical significance since it (1) describes the hardness of L2-approximate score modeling rather than exact representation, (2) directly targets the width, and (3) persists longer under OU flow. Both smoothness and Hermite rank can be viewed as notions of depth complexity of Ψ, since they obstruct modeling by shallow networks but not deep ones (depth can exponentially increase both 24 spatial irregularity and rank, but width cannot). By showing that the depth complexity of Ψ (which is fully present in the scores at τ = 0) propagates to the time-dependent scores along the OU flow, we make precise the intuition that “modeling the reverse OU drifts is as hard as modeling the full endpoint map for these pushforward distributions”. Hence, if Ψ has large depth complexity then the reverse OU transport fails to decompose it into simpler pieces. It is easy to construct examples for which an alternative transport succeeds in this goal. The obvious choice is to take Ψ itself to be a deep neural network, in which case the triangular shear map 

T from (59) is a deep ResNet and an alternative transport with shallow drifts may be built from the layers of the network itself. In the next two subsections, we will design such ResNet pushforwards to exhibit large third derivatives (by using deep networks to compose many nonlinearities) and high Hermite rank (by using wide three-layer networks and activations with heavy Hermite tails), which together yield Theorem 1. 

4.1.4 ResNet pushforwards: an exponential representation lower bound 

We first instantiate a triangular shear measure πΨ whose generating map Ψ has large third deriva-tives but admits a compositional decomposition into shallow pieces (and therefore a Barron-efficient alternative transport). This is accomplished by chaining together many nonlinearities across the layers of a deep network, which can amplify the nonlinearity to create derivatives that are expo-nential in the depth. Hence, we can construct a pushforward of γd by a ResNet of depth Θ( d) with two-layer blocks of width O(1) for which the following holds: 

Theorem 3 (Shallow representation lower bound, ResNet pushforward) . Fix d ≥ 4 and let ϕ(t) := max {0, t }3 be the ReLU 3 activation. Then, there exists a ResNet Tθ : Rd → Rd of depth Θ( d) and width O(1) (which uses a smoothed ReLU 3 activation) s.t. if πθ := ( Tθ)#γd ∈ P (Rd) then 

EBϕ (πθ) ≳ 2d, DBϕ (γd, π θ) ≲ d. (61)

The upper bound simply computes (62) using an ODE with single-index drifts (namely, the layers of Ψ θ), writes the result to the

Remark 5. The exponential separation result of (61) is fairly general: ω can be essentially any nondegenerate C5 

> b

function, ψ can have any tail behavior, ϕ can be any activation with bounded third derivatives which can represent ω and ψθ, the features need not be an orthonormal set (though 

25 they must be chained so that the output feature of each layer is the input feature of the next layer), and the lower bound is exponential in the depth (see Lemma 28 for a general statement). However, our proof that the scores are Barron relies heavily on the ReLU 3 structure (since we use a weighted-Sobolev-to-Barron embedding, see Lemma 20). This requires the right tails for ψ,which in turn requires choosing a teacher activation that can implement both ω and ψ.

In words, the distribution πθ encodes a sequence of very simple coordinatewise movements, and performing them in the correct order yields a generative model for πθ which can be implemented with small shallow neural network drifts. However, the reverse OU transport is unaware of this structure and attempts to adjust the complicated coordinate directly using Ψ θ, whose Barron norm increases exponentially with depth (the composition of Barron functions can cause an exponential increase in Barron norm, see [34, 78, 31]). Since the scores are Barron for all OU times, this exponential obstruction is quantitative rather than a statement that the scores escape the Barron space near the data side. −3 0 3

The cascade-across-coordinates structure in (62) is akin to the Gibbs scan, which is known in sampling theory as a way to sample complicated measures via simple iterated conditioning. These are the types of measures from which an algorithm like chained Langevin [21] is designed to sample. Our result shows that denoising models can fail to take advantage of this structure. 

4.1.5 ResNet pushforwards: an approximation lower bound 

The prior subsection showed that for a certain deep ResNet pushforward πθ, exact representation of the reverse OU/denoising generative model using shallow neural network drifts with polynomially-bounded weights requires exponential average width. That being said, we emphasize that the exponential obstruction we identify only occurs at exponentially-small OU times and relies on large third derivatives: a practical pipeline using early-stopping and L2-accurate score estimates can yield a generative model which approximates πθ without encountering the lower bound of Theorem 3. In this subsection we will remove both of these caveats with a construction for which any fixed-accuracy shallow drift approximation to the reverse OU generative model requires large width in high dimension. More precisely, we instantiate a triangular shear measure πΨ whose generating map Ψ has high Hermite rank and therefore cannot be efficiently approximated by shallow models. This is achieved by combining many independent features in a nonlinear way; the independent features create high rank, and a heavy Hermite tail of the nonlinear readout amplifies this effect by placing them in high Hermite degrees. We do so using a pushforward of γd by a ResNet of depth Θ( d) with two-layer blocks of width O(1), or alternatively by a three-layer network of width Θ( d). For notation, if πθ = ( Tθ)#γd ∈ P (Rd) is the Gaussian pushforward by a ResNet Tθ : Rd → Rd

with parameters θ then we denote by ρθτ := P ∗

and bθτ := ∇ log ρθτ 

> γd

for the OU scores w.r.t. Lebesgue and the Gaussian, respectively.

. Hence, a practical denoising sampling setup (using early-stopping and L2-accurate scores) which aims to achieve small error must model scores in this window and will therefore encounter our lower bound. More precisely, standard sampling guarantees for practical denoising models (e.g. [18, 16]) decompose the end-to-end sampling error along the lines of sampling error ≲ early-stopping error + L2 score-estimation error + discretization error . (65) In Lemma 32, we show that early-stopping at OU time τ ≍ ε4/3 incurs √KL error ≳ ε and both 

W1 and TV error ≳ ε4/3 for the hard instance of Theorem 4. Our lower bound therefore implies that a denoising setup using shallow network drifts which gets the first two terms of (65) to ≲ δ

requires width ≳ dpoly(1 /δ ). This is a more substantial hardness result than that of Theorem 3, since it implies that the practical denoising score-matching procedure will fail on these measures when using shallow drift networks of insufficient width. Theorem 4 is also different from the prior score-matching hardness works of [73, 81], since the obstruction does not come from computational/cryptographic difficulty nor spatial regularity but rather from the approximation capabilities of shallow neural networks. Instead of revealing an unavoidable issue that score-matching encounters, this construction highlights an approximation-theoretic separation between modeling scores with shallow and deep drift networks. In Section 5.2, we will discuss some conclusions and intuitions regarding the design of generative models which can be drawn from this result. 

Remark 8. The same construction with χ = g = tanh yields a similar result: for 0 ≤ τ ≲ ε, we get that m ≳ dc log(1 /ε )2

and ∥ˆsτ ∥Bϕ ≳ εd c 

> 2log(1 /ε )2

. To show this, we apply the other result from Lemma 31 to see that K(ε) ≳ log(1 /ε )2, after which we use Corollary 3. In this case, getting the first two terms of (65) to ≲ δ using shallow score networks requires width ≳ dΩ(log(1 /δ )2).

We finish up by showing a simpler integrated form of the lower bound, which is the one quoted in the informal statement of Theorem 1.

4.2 Feature learning separation 

We now aim to study dimensional separations between the DBϕ and rotation-equivariant DHk

distances as a way to understand the role of feature learning vs. learning drifts in a rotation-equivariant kernel space. We do this first in a perturbatively-small regime where matching upper and lower bounds are possible, after which we will present an initial result in the nonperturbative setting. These results together yield Theorem 2. We start in a simple setting where π is a small pushforward of a rotation-invariant measure by a single-index ReLU map: 

Example 2 (Perturbative feature learning, single-index ReLU pushforward) . Let d ≥ 4 and

w(x) := uϕ (⟨x, θ ⟩), νε := ( 1 + εw )#μ ∈ P 2(Rd). (69) 

Then, for any rotation-equivariant k ∈ K rot which satisfies Assumption k we have

Proof. See Section A.5. This is essentially a restatement of the folklore fact that “a single-index ReLU function does not lie in any rotation-invariant RKHS for d ≥ 4” (see [7, Appendix D]) in the setting of vector-valued functions and rotation-equivariant RKHS. For completeness we provide a proof by combining a first-order dual lower bound with a standard argument involving spherical harmonics; in the ReLU case the sum over harmonics diverges for d ≥ 4. 14 

The proof method via spherical harmonics (or Hermite decompositions when the source is Gaussian) is general, and can be used to show matching upper and lower bounds for perturbatively-small single-index flows (see Lemma 10 for a general statement). We apply it here to exhibit some quantitative separations for other activations:

Remark 9. The use of spherical harmonics to lower bound RKHS norms of single-index functions is well-known in the field; our only contribution to this story is the application of this technique to the local geometry of the DB metrics/the measure transport problem. 

Based on this initial perturbative result, it is tempting to conclude that “for small ε, there is the classical superpolynomial-in-d separation between rotation-equivariant DHk and DBϕ when 30 transporting to the endpoints of ε-length single-index flows”. Examples 2 and 3 indicate that this is the case when ε ↓ 0 with d fixed, but it is entirely possible that this separation only kicks in when 

ε is very tiny, say ε ≲ e−d. After all, by taking ε ↓ 0 we are effectively constraining a transport to use time-independent drifts, which does not allow an RKHS-based transport to leverage depth. 15 

It turns out that this is exactly the case: in the macroscopic/nonperturbative setting, there exist shortcuts which can be taken to lower the total RKHS energy to poly( d). More precisely, we construct an efficient alternate flow which matches the endpoint map of a time-invariant single-index flow by paying a one-time cost which is poly( d) and independent of ε. We demonstrate this mechanism in the simple setting where ⟨u, θ ⟩ = 0 and ϕ is smooth and bounded, but we expect it to apply more generally: 

Example 4 (Nonperturbative feature learning, autonomous single-index flows) . Let d ≥ 3, fix 

ϕ ∈ C∞ 

> b

(R) and u, θ ∈ Sd−1 with ⟨u, θ ⟩ = 0 , and define νT := Law( XT ) for the flow (71). Then, there exists a smooth rotation-equivariant k⋆ ∈ K rot (depending only on d, ϕ, T ) and a transport 

(vt, ρ t)t∈[0 ,1] from γd to νT using poly( d)-Lipschitz drifts which satisfies 

(

tr k⋆(x, x )dρ t(x)

∥vt∥Hk⋆ dt ≲ poly( d). (74) 

Proof. The precise nonasymptotic statement is given in Proposition 7, and it holds with γd replaced by any initial ρ0 ∈ P (Rd) for which ( ∫ 

> Rd

|x|ddρ 0(x)) 1/d ≲ poly( d). The method is similar to how the linear Stein metric achieves logarithmic dependence on the mean in Proposition 3: there is an initial compression/preconditioning phase in the direction u, followed by an exponentially-shrunk version of the original single-index transport, followed by a decompression phase. Since the compression/decompression phases have costs logarithmic in the compression factor, we may efficiently shrink the single-index phase to achieve polynomial total cost. Thus, endpoint maps of (time-invariant) single-index flows can be efficiently represented using drifts which lie in a rotation-equivariant RKHS. Taken literally, this result says that for ε ≳ 1poly( d)

we no longer have the superpolynomial separations between rotation-equivariant DHk and DBϕ that we saw in the ε ↓ 0 regime in Examples 2 and 3. More philosophically, it indicates depth can allow RKHS methods to model some functions which require feature learning in the shallow case. In the setting of generative modeling, this result lends some credibility to the idea of learning dynamical transports by representing drifts in RKHS, see e.g. [36, 65, 59]. Our investigation only scratches the surface of the comparison between the DBϕ and rotation-equivariant DHk metrics: all we have shown is that the classical shallow separations persist locally in the dynamical measure transport problem, but they break down once depth and time-dependent drifts are allowed. We consider it an important direction for future work to investigate the efficiency of modeling more complex data using drifts which lie in rotation-equivariant RKHS, particularly for targets which are flows/pushforwards by neural networks. 

# 5 Outlook 

In this paper, we introduced the Barron transport metric DBϕ as a technical tool to help us study approximation theory for generative modeling. In this section, we highlight some open questions and directions for future research as well as a potential application beyond generative modeling.      

> 15 For a more precise geometric perspective, by taking ε↓0 we are measuring the norm of the original single-index drift in the tangent spaces of the manifolds ( P(Rd), D Hk) and ( P(Rd), D Bϕ). By doing so, we reason about the RKHS norm of the prescribed transport instead of the infimum over all transports.

31 5.1 Algorithmic and computational ideas 

Our results can be summarized as follows: representing drifts with neural networks (which uti-lize feature learning) can provably allow dynamical transports with more efficient representations, but the standard denoising score-matching algorithm can fail to find such transports even in very natural cases. Similarly, it is folklore that algorithms which attempt to learn the Wasserstein optimal transport with neural networks can suffer from lack of drift regularity. 16 Hence, the cur-rent paradigms for learning dynamical generative models encounter neural network representation bottlenecks, even on easy data for which simple generative models with shallow drifts exist. This opens up the obvious next question: do there exist efficient algorithms which can (approx-imately) solve dynamical optimal transport problems like DHk or DBϕ given access to uncoupled samples from source and target measures? Such an algorithm would yield an approach to dynamical generative modeling which is built to seek efficient representation via kernel methods or neural net-works; this may prove useful on real-world data in high dimension. By contrast, the nonexistence of such an algorithm would yield some information about the fundamental difficulty of learning generative models from samples. Furthermore, given the expressivity of transports with RKHS drifts (see Example 4) it is also valuable to study the algorithmic differences between the DHk and 

DBϕ problems. The algorithmic/computational aspects of this question are poorly understood and constitute a valuable direction for future work. 

Remark 10 (Time regularity of geodesics) . An efficient algorithm to learn dynamical trans-ports must somehow discretize [0 , 1] , which relies on the regularity-in-time of the optimal transport 

(vt, ρ t)t∈[0 ,1] . At the current moment we do not have precise control on this (since the DHk and 

DBϕ energies do not directly penalize irregularity of t 7 → vt). A promising direction involves a priori estimates on the geodesic PDE. As an example, in the Stein case with a Mat´ ern/Sobolev kernel Hk ∼= Hs, the PDE (20) and Sobolev multiplication estimates ensure that any constant-speed DHk geodesic has sup t∈[0 ,1] ∥∂tvt∥Hs−1 ≲ DHk (ρ0, ρ 1).This approach will be formalized and developed for the general case in [28]. 

In light of the deep ResNet pushforward counterexamples from Theorems 3 and 4, we can formulate the following slightly more precise ResNet version of this question: 

Question 2 (Efficient generative modeling of ResNet pushforwards) . Given a teacher ResNet 

F : Rd → Rd and samples from its Gaussian pushforward π = F#γd, is there an efficient algorithm which learns a drift field (vt)t∈[0 ,1] transporting γd to π? In other words, can we efficiently learn a deep ResNet/neural ODE which maps γd to π? What properties of F allow such an algorithm? Ideally, we want the learned transport to have path norm/Barron cost comparable to that of F .

Remark 11 (Shifted transport metric) . To make this question more practical, it suffices to trans-port γd to a measure which approximates π in some metric or divergence. Allowing the target to be shifted within a small ball can substantially facilitate transport; this tradeoff between controlled met-ric displacement and dynamical transport is closely related to the shifted composition methodology of [4]. In our setting, this would correspond to the quantity 

> ˜

DB,ε (μ, π ) = inf  

> {∫ 10

> ∫10

> L2(ρt;Rd)

dt ≤ ε2

> }
