> 16 See the counterexample of Caffarelli [14], which suffers from a discontinuous optimal transport map due to the target’s nonconvex support. Any ODE with e.g. Lipschitz drifts cannot represent such a map, and so neither can any flow model with Barron drifts using a Lipschitz activation.

32 where the infimum is over transports (vt, ρ t)t∈[0 ,1] satisfying ∂tρt + ∇ · (vtρt) = 0 and ρ0 = μ, ρ 1 = π.Under mild regularity of ˆvt (e.g. Lipschitz regularity, see [3, Prop. 3]), the drift approximation can be translated into Wasserstein control of the transport endpoint W2(ˆ π, π ) ≲ ε.

There are some strong prior works due to Chen et al. regarding the computational hardness of learning pushforwards of the Gaussian by shallow neural networks: in [20] they show that a Gaussian pushed forward by a two-layer neural network with polynomial activations can be efficiently learned under either quadratic or low-rank structure, while in [19] they show by a moment-matching argument that a Gaussian pushed forward by a two-layer ReLU neural network with log( d)hidden neurons cannot always be efficiently learned in the statistical query model. These results mirror the regression setting, where small Barron norm of a target function does not necessarily imply efficient learnability [26, Theorem 4] but a suitable sparse 17 neural network representation or special activation structure does (this is the subject of recent work on the multi-index model, see e.g. the survey [13]). Based on this, we can pose a question simpler than Question 2 as a concrete next step toward understanding the computational landscape of generative modeling: 

Question 3 (Efficient generative modeling of multi-index pushforwards) . Given a teacher multi-index vector field F : Rd → Rd of the form F (x) = ∑mj=1 σj uj ϕ(⟨x, θ j ⟩) and samples from its Gaussian pushforward π = (Id + F )#γd, is there an efficient algorithm which learns a generative model for π? Which properties of F allow such an algorithm (e.g. m = O(1) )? 

When m = 1 this problem is closely related to non-Gaussian component analysis (NGCA), which is known to exhibit statistical-computational gaps; see [25] and references therein. The result of Example 1 shows that the standard denoising algorithm will have difficulty representing the reverse OU drifts of such a target using two-layer neural networks, 18 but the overall computational hardness of this problem is not completely understood. A key difference from the multi-index regression setting is that a learner can only observe moments from the π marginal as opposed to cross-moments of the induced coupling of ( γd, π ), which makes it easier to hide information by matching moments. We leave the investigation into Question 3 for future work. 

5.2 The role of depth and layerwise supervision 

In a practical generative model, we are constrained by the amount of inference-time computation required to generate a sample. Consider a dynamical generative model (a flow/SDE model perform-ing e.g. denoising or optimal transport), and suppose that the drifts are modeled using ResNets. The layers of each ResNet can be viewed as a sequence of separate drifts in their own right: from the perspective of a particle, any update of the form x 7 → x + ηF (x) is a “drift”. The natural quantity measuring sequential computation is the number of drift evaluations times the number of layers in each drift network, since this captures how many basic operations each particle must undergo during generation. The major difference lies in how the transport is learned: each drift ResNet is trained to perform a specific operation (e.g. in denoising models they attempt to match the reverse OU drifts), while an individual layer of such a drift ResNet may implement any subroutine to help. Put simply, we may view the entire generative process as a single very deep ResNet which we train by supervised regression on various intermediate layers of the practitioner’s choosing. Indeed, once the total depth is fixed by computational constraints, it is up to the designer whether to 

> 17 In fact, in the regression setting the sparsity threshold for poly( d) complexity is also log( d) hidden neurons without further assumptions, which aligns with the lower bound of [19].
> 18 Note that a flow by a single-index ReLU vector field can be represented by a single-index ReLU pushforward due to homogeneity, and vice versa.

33 allocate more computation toward deeper drift models or more drift evaluations per generation; this is equivalent to less or more layerwise supervision, respectively. This raises a natural algorithmic question, which we pose for denoising models specifically: 

Question 4 (Allocation of depth/layerwise supervision) . For a fixed total depth of the generative process of a denoising model, what is the “best” allocation between the number of neural network score evaluations and the depth of each score neural network? Here, “best” should be measured in terms of both training complexity and sample quality. 

The two extremes are (1) minimal supervision, in which case we train one deep ResNet push-ing the Gaussian to the target distribution and (2) maximal supervision, in which case there are many shallow networks trained via score-matching. The first approach is reminiscent of generative adversarial networks (GANs) [39]; although these have been famously outperformed by iterative dynamical methods such as diffusion models [24], recently there has been much progress in mini-mizing the number of denoising steps required during generation [68, 74, 48]. In Theorems 3 and 4 we showed the potential suboptimality of the second approach, since shallow score networks can cause inefficiencies due to the nature of the reverse OU supervision. The optimal allocation is likely data-dependent and somewhere in the middle of these two extremes, and it is a useful direction of future work to understand the underlying tradeoffs in both simple and real-world settings. 

5.3 Application to sampling 

We defined the Barron transport metric DBϕ as a dynamical optimal transport problem with tangent norm ∥ · ∥ Bϕ . It turns out that any choice of tangent norm will yield a (Finsler) manifold geometry on P(Rd), with an associated theory of absolutely continuous curves, geodesics, and gradient flows in terms of the continuity equation. Importantly, the gradient flow of KL( ·∥ π) w.r.t. such a geometry will always yield an algorithm for sampling from π given query access to ∇ log π.This is the perspective underlying modern analysis of the SVGD sampling algorithm as a DHk

gradient flow (see [30]), as well as the recently-introduced Radon-Wasserstein geometry and its induced sampling algorithm [42]. In the companion paper [28], we leverage this observation to design other sampling algorithms through careful selection of the tangent norms. Doing so will require a rigorous study of the optimal transport geometry of these generalized dynamical metrics in the spirit of Ambrosio-Gigli-Savar´ e[5]. In this subsection we will ignore mathematical rigor and the full generality, and instead we give a taste of the sampling application in the case of the Barron transport metric DBϕ in order to further motivate the study of the ( P(Rd), D Bϕ ) geometry. Indeed, by taking the gradient flow of KL( ·∥ π) in the metric space ( P(Rd), D Bϕ ) (in the sense of [5, Chapter 1]) and discretizing it, we arrive at the following algorithm for sampling from π ∝ e−V

with potential V : Rd → R via a cloud of interacting particles: This interacting particle sampler regresses a single-index vector field at each step, along which it coherently flows all the particles. This is very similar in spirit to the SVGD algorithm, except that the velocity field is selected in a way which utilizes feature learning instead of kernel regression. Algorithm 1 has favorable behavior on target distributions which have hidden low-dimensional structure. As some illustrative examples which are strongly log-concave: (1) (Hidden subspace). If our target is π(2) ∝ e−aψ (⟨x,θ ⟩)−∥ x∥2/2 for some hidden a > 0, θ ∈ Sd−1,and an even C2 ψ : R → R for which π(2) is strongly log-concave, then the following hold when initializing from γd assuming positive finite initial KL: • LMC with any deterministic, nonnegative step size schedule requires Ω( √d) steps to decrease KL by a constant factor. • Mean-field SVGD with a Gaussian or Mat´ ern kernel and any bounded stable step size schedule requires Ω( d) steps to decrease KL by a constant factor. • The mean-field Barron transport sampler (with activation 20 ϕ = ψ′ and a dimension-independent step size) achieves KL ≤ ε in O(log(1 /ε )) steps. Here a, ψ , and the step size bounds are independent of dimension, and Gaussian and Mat´ ern kernels are normalized to tr k(x, x ) = d. This behavior is numerically verified in Figure 4. In words, the Barron transport sampler is provably useful for discovering hidden directions and choosing adaptive step sizes in those directions, whereas other samplers can suffer from trying to apply similar step sizes in all directions or in a fixed feature basis. This phenomenon persists for more complex target measures sharing these properties (multi-index tilts, products of these examples, etc.). Indeed, one can show dimension-free convergence rates for the discretized DBϕ

sampler on log-smooth targets satisfying a log-Sobolev inequality for which the relative scores along the sampling path have small Barron norm. We leave the derivation of this update rule, convergence rates, and the math/intuition behind the above examples to the companion paper [28].

This section contains the proofs of various claims from the main paper.

In this subsection we prove some general lower bounds on the dynamical transport distances: namely, if B ↪→ Cb(Rd) with sup norm then W∞ ≲ DB while if B ↪→ W 1,∞(Rd) then KL ≲ DB +D2

> B

for sufficiently regular endpoints. These do not play a role in the results of the main paper, but they are useful tools and offer interesting consequences for our generative modeling applications. 

Lemma 3 (Wasserstein and KL control) . Let B be a Banach space of functions from Rd → Rd s.t. 

∥w∥∞ ≤ M0∥w∥B (∀w ∈ B ) (108) 

for some M0 < ∞. Then, for every p ∈ [1 , ∞] we have 

Wp(μ, ν ) ≤ M0DB(μ, ν ) (∀μ, ν ∈ P (Rd)) . (109) 

Suppose further that B ↪→ W 1,∞ continuously and 

∥ div w∥L∞ ≤ M1∥w∥B (∀w ∈ B ) (110) 

for some M1 < ∞. Let μ ∝ e−V be s.t. ∇V is β-Lipschitz and Sμ := Eμ∥∇ V ∥ < ∞. Then, 

KL( ν∥μ) ≤ (M0Sμ + M1)DB(μ, ν ) + βM 20

2 DB(μ, ν )2 (∀ν ∈ P (Rd)) . (111) 

Proof. Fix an admissible ( vt, ρ t)t∈[0 ,1] ∈ S μ→ν and write L := ∫ 10 ∥vt∥Bdt . By the superposition principle for the weak continuity equation, there is a probability measure Q on absolutely continuous curves ω : [0 , 1] → Rd s.t. ( et)#Q = ρt and ˙ ωt = vt(ωt) for Q-a.e. ω. Hence,

A.3 Gaussian-to-Gaussian transport 

We first analyze the optimal and reverse OU transports in the setting where the source and target are both Gaussian. For W2 optimal transport and reverse OU the drifts are always affine, which means that intermediate measures remain Gaussian and so we can analyze mean and covariance dynamics explicitly without relying on perturbative arguments. For the DHk and DBϕ settings we must impose this structure ourselves, i.e. restrict to kernels and activations which are affine. For simplicity, throughout this subsection we take γd = N (0 , 1d) the standard Gaussian and 

π = N (m, Σ) an arbitrary nondegenerate Gaussian with Σ ≻ 0. The general case should follow through similar methods. 

A.3.1 Optimal transport distances 

First, we recall that the Wasserstein distance has an exact form for Gaussians:

Remark 12. The transports realizing the upper bounds for linear Stein and linear Barron are almost the same: they both use the triangle inequality (i.e. they move the mean and covariance in separate steps) and they use the exact same transport to move the covariance. Furthermore, the transports which move the mean both use a compress-translate-expand strategy to achieve logarithmic dependence on ∥m∥. This is exactly analogous to the structure of a hyperbolic geodesic, and it matches the fact that hyperbolic distance between horizontally-separated points is logarithmic in the Euclidean separation.

(431) These results describe a superpolynomial separation between the Barron and rotation-equivariant Stein transport metrics along single-index flows in the perturbatively small regime. To summarize, we have shown that if νε := Law( Xε) for the time-invariant single-index flow ˙Xt = uϕ (⟨Xt, θ ⟩), X0 ∼ γd, (432) then for any ϕ ∈ { ReLU , tanh , sin } and any rotation-equivariant k ∈ K rot satisfying Assumption k

(433) 79 if d ≥ 4 and ⟨u, θ ⟩̸ = 0, and that these scalings are sharp in the sense that there are rotation-equivariant kernels matching them. We emphasize that in this perturbative setting we are effectively constraining transports to use time-independent drifts, thereby linearizing the problem and reducing to control of the RKHS norms of single-index functions. This result makes it tempting to claim that “for small ε, there is a superpolynomial-in-d sepa-ration between rotation-equivariant DHk and Barron DBϕ in terms of the representation of ε-length single-index flows”. By (433) this is true as ε ↓ 0 with d fixed. However, this has the potential to be misleading: the superpolynomial separation may only kick in when ε is smaller than any 1poly( d) .In fact, it turns out that this is the case. More precisely, the next result shows that 

ε ≳ 1poly( d) =⇒

DBϕ (γd, ν ε) ≲ poly( d) (434) for some rotation-equivariant k ∈ K rot satisfying Assumption k. Put differently, we can construct an alternate flow which matches the endpoint map of a time-invariant single-index flow by paying a one-time cost which is poly( d) and independent of ε. We demonstrate this mechanism in the simplest setting of shears, i.e. single-index flows with ⟨u, θ ⟩ = 0:

Remark 14. This shortcut construction uses the same compress-transport-expand strategy that the optimal transports used in the Gaussian endpoints and linear drift case, see Remark 12. In that setting it was optimal up to universal constants, but in this nonlinear setting we expect there to be more efficient shortcuts. 

A.6 Reverse OU (sub)optimality for single-index flows and tilts

Proof. Set ˆΘ := Rd × R to be the parameter space. If ϕ is bounded, let Θ := ˆΘ ∪ {∞} be its one-point compactification and define

In the main paper, we present lower bounds for the representation and approximation by two-layer neural networks of the OU scores of certain ResNet pushforwards. These proof techniques extend to any measure with a triangular shear structure, which may be of wider interest. We study the general case in this section, and in Section A.8 we specialize these general lower bounds to the ResNet pushforward examples.

In words, our representation result (Corollary 2) lower bounds the third derivatives of the scores (and therefore the Barron norm for sufficiently regular ϕ) in terms of the derivatives of Ψ. The approximation result (Proposition 9) is stronger, as it leverages the spectral tails of the Hermite tensor decomposition of Ψ in order to lower bound the width of any shallow L2 approximation; this can be converted to a lower bound on the Barron norm of the approximation. In both cases we allow the added flexibility of ψ different from the identity: in the representation case we allow ψ to have nonlinear tails (in which case the last coordinate of πψ, Ψ has non-Gaussian tails), and in the approximation case we allow ψ to be a scalar multiple of the identity (in which case the last coordinate of πψ, Ψ is Ψ( Z) smoothed by a Gaussian of any fixed variance). To help interpret these lower bounds, we also demonstrate some settings where the correspond-ing upper bounds hold in a nonquantitative sense: 

Theorem 6 (Shallow representation and approximation upper bounds, nonquantitative) . Fix ψ, Ψ

and define sψ, Ψ

, b ψ, Ψ

as above. Then, the following hold: (i) If Ψ( z) = ∑Mr=1 Φr(PEr z) for subspaces Er ⊆ Rn with Φr ∈ C∞ 

> c

(Er) and ψ is a smooth increasing diffeomorphism with ψ(u) ∼ up outside of a compact set for some p > 2, then 

sψ, Ψ

∈ B ReLU s for any s ∈ N and all τ ≥ 0.(ii) For any ψ, Ψ, any ε, τ > 0, and any nonpolynomial activation ϕ, there is some two-layer neu-ral network ˆsτ of finite width which uses activation ϕ and satisfies ∥sψ, Ψ 

> τ

replaced by bψ, Ψ

In words, we provide conditions under which the OU-smoothed scores sψ, Ψ 

> τ

, b ψ, Ψ 

> τ

are (i) them-selves Barron and therefore representable by infinite-width two-layer neural networks of finite path norm (Lemma 21) and (ii) are approximable to arbitrary L2 accuracy by finite-width two-layer neural networks (Proposition 10). These are proved by a Sobolev-to-Barron embedding (Lemmas 19 and 20) and by the classic universal L2 approximation result of Hornik [44], respectively. We do not provide quantitative upper bounds on the widths or Barron norms; these results merely serve as sanity checks to complement our quantitative lower bounds. 

A.7.1 Representation lower bound via third derivative 

We first lower bound the homogeneous Sobolev seminorm ∥ · ∥ ˙W 3,∞(Rn+1 ;Rn+1 ) of the drifts and their integrated value E ˙W 3,∞(Rn+1 ;Rn+1 )(πψ, Ψ). The mechanism is elementary: if Ψ is smooth and bounded with large derivatives and ψ is the identity near the origin, then a standard OU stability argument transfers those large derivatives to sψ, Ψ 

> τ

(688) If instead ϕ is 3-homogeneous as in (ii), then the same argument works since ∥ϕ′′′ ∥L∞ < ∞.We also verify that the scores can lie in the Barron space under sufficient smoothness and tail behavior. This is a sanity check to ensure that the result of Corollary 2 is quantitative and not simply a disguised version of the statement that “the scores are not Barron”. First, we define a class of functions for which this is simple to verify: 

Lemma 19 (Cylindrical Schwartz functions are Barron w.r.t. ReLU s). Let n ∈ N and λ ∈ (0 , ∞).We define Cλ to be the class of functions f : Rn × R → R which can be written as a finite sum 

f (z, u ) = 

> M

∑

> r=1

Fr(PEr z, u ) (689) 

where each Er ⊆ Rn is a linear subspace (with orthogonal projection PEr ) and each Fr : Er ×R → R

satisfies the seminorm bounds 

|∂αx ∂juFr(x, u )| ≲α,j,N (1 + ∥x∥)−N (1 + |u|)−λ−j (690) 

for all N ∈ N, all multi-indices α, and all j ∈ N ∪ { 0}.The class Cλ is an algebra which is closed under smooth composition and OU smoothing, i.e. the following properties hold: 

104 (i) If f, g ∈ C λ, then f g ∈ C λ with seminorms controlled by those of f, g and the angles between the involved subspaces. (ii) If f ∈ C λ and η ∈ C∞(R) satisfies η(0) = 0 and all derivatives of η are bounded, then 

η ◦ f ∈ C λ with seminorms controlled by those of f , the derivative bounds on η, and the angles between the involved subspaces. (iii) If f ∈ C λ and g(z, u ) = EG∼γn [f (az + bG, u )] for some 0 < a − ≤ a ≤ a+ < ∞ and 0 ≤ b ≤

b+ < ∞, then g ∈ C λ with seminorms controlled by those of f with constants depending only on a−, a +, b +, and subspace dimensions. Furthermore, for any s ∈ N with ϕ(t) = max {0, t }s the ReLU s activation and Bϕ its associated Barron space of functions from Rn+1 → R (using the scalar version of Definition 4), we have

Throughout this subsection we will take ψ(u) = σu for some σ > 0 (i.e. ψ(U ) is centered Gaussian) and focus on the dependence on Ψ. We will now lower bound the widths and Barron norms of two-layer neural network approximations to the scores sψ, Ψ 

> τ

/b ψ, Ψ 

> τ

, as opposed to exact representations. This approach is more robust, leveraging high Hermite rank of Ψ to directly lower bound the width of any sum of ridge functions approximating the score. The technique borrows largely from the recent paper [12], and we adapt the general lower bound method to the reverse OU application. Let us first define a class of functions which contains two-layer neural networks of width m.Since the Hermite method controls width in a general sense, the neurons can be very general ridge functions which need not share an activation function. 

Definition 7 (Shallow function class) . For each n, m ∈ N, we define the class R(n)

We will make use of some notation from Gaussian/Hermite analysis; for references, see [47, Definition 2.1, Example 3.18, Theorem 4.1] of the textbook of Janson. Fix n ∈ N and let Ψ : Rn → R

Definition 9 (Truncated Gaussian averaging operator) . Fix R > 0. Let F : Rn+1 → Rn+1 be a measurable vector field. Using coordinates (z, u ) ∈ Rn+1 with z ∈ Rn and u ∈ R, assume that 

Fn+1 (z, ·) is integrable w.r.t. γ1 on [−R, R ] for a.e. z, and define ARF : Rn → R by 

(ARF )( z) := EU ∼γ1 [Fn+1 (z, U ) | | U | ≤ R]. (793) 

We call AR the averaging operator with truncation R.

A.7.3 Early-stopping lower bounds 

The presented lower bounds describe the difficulty of score-matching using shallow neural networks for OU times in a certain window τ ∈ [0 , τ 0]. However, in practice it is common to avoid modeling scores all the way to τ ↓ 0 by applying a cutoff. Qualitatively speaking, to approximate the target distribution π to accuracy δ it suffices to fix τ1 > 0 for which the distance between P ∗ 

> τ1

π and π is 

≲ δ and to only learn approximate scores ∥sτ − ˆsτ ∥L2(ρτ ) ≲ δ for τ ∈ [τ1, ∞), see e.g. [18]. Since we have shown that approximating scores on [0 , τ 0] with shallow networks is hard, it remains to show that choosing τ1 > τ 0 introduces a lot of early-stopping error. We will provide some general tools for triangular shears here, which we apply to our ResNet pushforward examples in Section A.8. We start with a lower bound on total variation and W1 distances which uses only the triangular shear structure. For μ, ν ∈ P (Rd), we set TV( μ, ν ) = sup |μ(A) − ν(A)| over measurable sets A.

A.8 Reverse OU suboptimality for ResNet pushforwards 

In the previous section, we dealt with the general class of Gaussian triangular shear measures 

πψ, Ψ = Law( Z, ψ (U ) + Ψ( Z)) ∈ P (Rn+1 ), (Z, U ) ∼ γn+1 (880) for ψ : R → R and Ψ : Rn → R. For this class, we developed some tools to get lower and upper bounds for the representation and approximation of the reverse OU scores sψ, Ψ 

> τ

, b ψ, Ψ 

> τ

by two-layer neural networks. In this section, we will (1) describe a very natural subclass of πψ, Ψ given by pushforwards of γn+1 by certain ResNets and (2) verify that the induced shear maps ( ψ, Ψ) satisfy the assumptions needed to apply the general lower and upper bounds. Our class of ResNets will have blocks which each perform a shear operation (i.e. x 7 → x +

uϕ (⟨x, θ ⟩) with ⟨u, θ ⟩ = 0). In words, the architecture is: 1. First, there are L layers which separately perform computation on the first n coordinates (labeled z) and on the last coordinate (labeled u). We denote the input by ( z(0) , u (0) ) ∈ Rn+1 ,and after layer ℓ we denote the hidden state by ( z(ℓ), u (ℓ)) ∈ Rn+1 . The computations in the 

z and u channels are performed independently. After these L layers, the value in the u

coordinate is u(L) = ψ(u(0) ). 123 2. Next, there is a “readout” layer which uses the state z(L) of the z coordinate to compute Ψ and add its output to the u coordinate. After this layer, the last coordinate contains 

u(L+1) = ψ(u(0) ) + Ψ( z(0) ) while the z coordinate remains z(L+1) = z(L).3. Lastly, there are another L layers which only act on the z coordinate and leave the u coordinate unchanged. These layers implement the inverses of the original L layers in reverse order. In particular, u(2 L+1) = u(L+1) and z(2 L+1) = z(0) .Combining these three stages produces a neural network of depth 2 L + 1 whose input-output map 

F : Rn+1 → Rn+1 is given by F (z(0) , u (0) ) = ( z(0) , ψ (u(0) ) + Ψ( z(0) )). Hence, F#γn+1 = πψ, Ψ. This architecture allows us to represent πψ, Ψ as a ResNet pushforward of the Gaussian assuming that we can (1) implement Ψ using L layers of single-index shears in the z channel along with a readout layer and (2) implement ψ using L layers in the u channel. The shear structure of the z channel is crucial, since it allows us to invert the entire stage. As an added bonus, it will let us represent each layer using a continuous-time flow. We now introduce notation for this construction. For an activation φ : R → R and a width 

m ∈ N, we define a class of functions from R → R by 

Nφ,m := 

x 7 → c0 +

> m

∑

> j=1

cj φ(αj x + βj ); (cj )mj=0 , (αj )mj=1 , (βj )mj=1 ⊆ R

 . (881) These are the functions which can be implemented by width-m neural networks with activation φ.Any ˜φ ∈ N φ,m can be treated as a compatible activation, in the sense that any layer of width w

with activation ˜φ can be implemented using a layer of width wm with activation φ.

Definition 10 (Triangular ResNet) . Fix an activation φ : R → R and n, W, L ∈ N. Choose scalar functions g, χ ∈ N φ,W . For each ℓ ∈ { 1, . . . , L }, choose hℓ ∈ N φ,W and input/output features 

aℓ, b ℓ ∈ Rn with ⟨aℓ, b ℓ⟩ = 0 . Choose also a readout direction v ∈ Sn−1. Using these choices, we define a ResNet: 1. With (z(0) , u (0) ) ∈ Rn+1 the input, the first stage of L layers is 

z(ℓ) = z(ℓ−1) + bℓg

We use the notation “TResNet” to emphasize the triangular structure, since we compute the z and u coordinates separately, combine their outputs, and roll the z computation back to its initial state. Remark 19. Note that this obstruction is proved only for exponentially-small OU times. We create small but very rapid variations, which amplify the third derivative but become hidden under a small amount of noise. 

We may also ensure that the Barron norms of the OU scores are finite for the examples produced in Lemma 28. A.8.2 Approximation lower bounds 

We will apply Proposition 9 for a shallow approximation lower bound of the OU scores sθτ , b θτ . To do so, we require a lower bound on the Hermite width envelope mΨθ , which is defined in terms of spectral tails of the Hermite modes (see Definition 8). First, we demonstrate that three-layer neural networks of the form (947) produce heavy spectral tails in high Hermite degrees. The mechanism is Hermite branching: the nonlinear Hermite modes of 

g generate many independent tensor factors along the orthogonal feature directions, producing high-rank Hermite matricizations. A heavy Hermite tail of χ ensures that these high-rank obstructions occur at high Hermite degrees, where the rank can be large. Recalling the definition of aΨ from Definition 8 and that He j denote the monic probabilist’s Hermite polynomials, a precise statement is given below: 131 Lemma 30 (Hermite branching lemma) .
